    EvoSkill: teach an agent a reusable skill¶

    What it does. EvoSkill discovers reusable skills from an agent's failures, keeping the underlying model fixed.

    How it works. An executor attempts tasks; a proposer diagnoses failures and suggests skills; a generator writes the skill folders. New folders are evaluated before selection. The paper retains a Pareto frontier using validation performance.

    In this example. Watch three Claude agents answer OfficeQA questions, propose and build a native SKILL.md, then retry a missed question. This small adaptation uses Population(size=3) and a final held-out comparison; it does not reproduce the paper's search or benchmark results.

    Experimental/live-only.

    Role Here
    Artifact A folder of reusable skill files
    Proposer revise: diagnose failures, propose a skill, then build its files
    Evaluator evaluate: run the executor and apply the independent OfficeQA scorer
    Search Population chooses which folder to revise next
    Result Selected skills, candidate history, SDK receipts, and held-out comparison

    Run one cell at a time. The first six code cells show the agent workflow; the last two run the longer search and held-out check. Setup fetches two readable Python helpers and pinned data automatically. Use a Python 3.12+ kernel with pip on macOS or Linux, authenticated with claude auth login or ANTHROPIC_API_KEY. Run All makes at most 25 paid SDK calls and can take tens of minutes.

    Download notebook · SDK options and callable · Grading and folder helpers

    For the script, install examples/research/evoskill/requirements.txt and run uv run --no-sync python examples/research/evoskill/main.py.

    Saved live run: 2026-09-21, Claude SDK 0.2.156. Outputs below are captured agent responses; rerunning makes new paid calls. The capture used 25 SDK calls over 35 minutes, with an SDK cost estimate of $3.19. The final notebook was verified by replaying these same responses: all prompts, scores and selected skill bytes matched.

    %pip install -q https://sentient-xyz.github.io/meta-evolve-docs/downloads/meta-evolve.zip claude-agent-sdk==0.2.156 pydantic==2.12.5
import json
from pathlib import Path
import tempfile

from pydantic import BaseModel, Field
import meta_evolve as meta
from urllib.request import urlretrieve
import sys

source = next((p / "examples/research/evoskill" for p in [Path.cwd(), *Path.cwd().parents]
               if (p / "examples/research/evoskill/evoskill_agents.py").is_file()), None)
if source is None:
    source = Path(tempfile.mkdtemp(prefix="evoskill-source-"))
    base = "https://sentient-xyz.github.io/meta-evolve-docs/downloads/evoskill/d547a7259a6b518d/"
    for name in ('evoskill_agents.py', 'evoskill_officeqa.py', 'resources.json', 'skill-builder/SKILL.md'):
        target = source / name
        target.parent.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)
        urlretrieve(base + name, target)
sys.path.insert(0, str(source))

from IPython.display import HTML, JSON, Markdown, display
from evoskill_agents import ClaudeAgent, agent_options, failure_feedback, transcripts
from evoskill_officeqa import (
    answer_table, evaluation, generated_skills, population_table, prepare, restore,
    save_report, skill_markdown, skill_revision, snapshot,
)

root = Path(tempfile.mkdtemp(prefix="evoskill-"))
data = prepare(root)
skill_repo = data.root / "skill-repo"
empty_skills = dict(snapshot(skill_repo))
print("Run folder:", data.root)

    Note: you may need to restart the kernel to use updated packages.
Run folder: /private/var/folders/fn/fg595s6s7wd8d9z3jhr5vsbw0000gn/T/evoskill-i672b9f9

    Components: three callable agents¶

    executor(task) returns an Answer; proposer(feedback) returns a SkillProposal. Both use the Claude SDK's native JSON-schema outputs. The generator loads the fixed skill-builder skill and writes a real SKILL.md.

    The options include setting_sources=["project"] and add_dirs=[skill_repo, ...]: the added repository contains .claude/skills/. Each call starts a fresh session so the next executor discovers newly written skills.

    class Answer(BaseModel):
    model_config = {"extra": "forbid"}
    final_answer: float = Field(description="One value in the question's requested units")
    explanation: str = Field(description="Brief explanation with the source table")


class SkillProposal(BaseModel):
    model_config = {"extra": "forbid", "str_strip_whitespace": True}
    name: str = Field(pattern=r"^[a-z][a-z0-9-]{0,62}$")
    description: str = Field(min_length=20, max_length=800)

options = agent_options(data, executor_model="haiku", skill_model="sonnet")
executor = ClaudeAgent(options["executor"], output_type=Answer, name="executor")
proposer = ClaudeAgent(options["proposer"], output_type=SkillProposal, name="proposer")
generator = ClaudeAgent(options["generator"], name="generator")

    1. Ask Haiku and inspect the answers¶

    All three questions ask for millions of nominal dollars:

    • UID0003 — Monthly sum: total national-defense expenditures across the twelve months of 1953.
    • UID0012 — Department total: spending by the highest-spending federal department in fiscal 1955.
    • UID0045 — Country lookup: total U.S. claims in 1997 on the country formerly called Zaire.

    The table shows each answer beside OfficeQA's ground truth; expand a trace to inspect the actual tool calls. Only final_answer reaches the official scorer. Its explanation stays separate.

    One explicit task clarification: UID0003 names Table 2 of the February 1954 Treasury Bulletin. Tables 2 and 3 print conflicting monthly values; the official answer matches Table 2. The original question and this addition are recorded. The gold answer and official scorer are unchanged.

    cases = data.cases("development")
before = [executor(case["question"]).require() for case in cases]
display(Markdown(answer_table(cases, before, data.score_answer)))
display(HTML(transcripts(before)))
feedback = evaluation(cases, before, data.score_answer, "development")
if feedback.failure:
    raise RuntimeError(str(feedback.failure))
failures = failure_feedback(feedback.evidence[0].data)

Effective January 1, 1951, the old-age insurance taxes on employers and employees and the withheld income tax are paid into the Treasury in combined amounts without separation as to type of tax. Figures for June 1953 include adjustments by increase in amounts transferred as appropriations as follows: $53 million based on records of self-employment income for calendar year 1951 and $39 million based on records of wages earned in the quarters ending September 30 and December 31, 1952. Further back in the production chain there are no signs of building price pressures, with producer prices continuing to decline at all three stages of\n292:During the first 10 months of the year, the consumer price index (CPI) rose at a seasonally adjusted annual rate of 1.6 percent, close to the 1.7 percent gain last year but well below the 3.3 percent advance posted in 1996. Declining energy prices\n296:[Omitted long matching line]\n300:quarters of the year, faster than the 1.9 percent increase in productivity. This has pushed unit labor costs up at a 2.1 percent annual rate, the same as registered during 1997. The employment cost index for total compensation rose by 1.0 percent for the 3 months ending in September, up from 0.7 percent and 0.9 percent, respectively, for the 3-month periods ending in March and June. Over the last 12 months the index has risen by 3.7 percent, the largest increase in 6-1/2 years.\n308:[Omitted long matching line]\n328:Since even the high rate of income growth was outpaced by spending, the share of after-tax income devoted to personal saving fell to an extremely low 0.2 percent in the third quarter from 1.2 percent in the first quarter and 2.1 percent averaged in 1997. Huge gains in wealth associated with rising stock prices appear to have caused households to feel less need to save out of current income in recent years.\n332:Industrial production in manufacturing, mining and utilities has grown by a modest 0.4 percent annual rate during the first 10 months of 1998, a distinct slowdown from increases averaging 5.2 percent during the previous 2 years.\n336:[Omitted long matching line]\n340:[Omitted long matching line]\n342:[Omitted long matching line]\n346:Productivity in manufacturing has grown at a 2.4 percent annual rate so far during 1998, roughly half as fast as the 4-1/2 percent rate averaged during the previous 3 years. Unit labor costs in manufacturing have risen at only a 0.8 percent annual rate through the third quarter, about the same as last year. During the previous 3 years, these costs had fallen by 1.8 percent annually on average.\n350:[Omitted long matching line]\n352:Over the past several years, U.S. economic growth has outpaced that of our major trading partners. That caused imports to grow much more rapidly than exports. The sharp appreciation of the dollar in more recent years has widened the trade gap further. In addition, what had been a positive balance on investment income shifted to a negative in 1997 as the inflow of capital into the United States boosted payments of earnings and interest to foreigners.\n370:Financial crises in several newly industrialized Asian countries in 1997, and in other parts of the world this year, led in some cases to currency devaluation, raising the dollar's value against those currencies. One result was to make imports from\n372:those countries cheaper in dollar terms, a factor which has contributed to the low inflation performance in the United States. Another result was to raise the relative price of U.S. exports, which contributed to a decline in exports this year and is likely to continue to act as a drag on U.S. export growth through at least 1999.\n376:[Omitted long matching line]\n409:The Federal budget posted a surplus of $70 billion in fiscal 1998, the first surplus since 1969 and only the ninth since\n417:Strong economic growth and passage of deficit reduction programs placed the deficit on a downward course since 1992. In that year the deficit recorded an all-time high of $290 billion, then shrank to just $22 billion by fiscal 1997 before the budget moved into surplus in the year just ended.\n419:Growth in receipts has been very strong over the past 6 years, boosted by expansion in jobs and income, rising corporate profits, and stock market gains. In fiscal 1998, receipts rose by 9 percent, with an especially notable jump for nonwithheld in-\n427:dividual income taxes, up almost 13 percent over the year. Withheld income and employment taxes grew by 9 percent in fiscal 1998, reflecting strong growth in wages and salaries and employment.\n429:Growth in Federal spending was held to a moderate 3.2 percent in fiscal 1998, or even less after adjusting for some special financial flows which artificially reduced outlays in the previous fiscal year. Excluding large spectrum auction proceeds and asset sales in the deposit insurance account in 1997, both of which are treated as negative outlays in budget accounting, outlay growth in 1998 was just 2.3 percent.\n433:Net national saving has been rising steadily from a post-World War II low of 3.4 percent of net national product (NNP) in 1992 to 7.6 percent in the first half of 1998 (latest available). Despite the progress, net saving, which excludes depreciation to replace worn-out or obsolete equipment and structures used in production, still remains well below the 12.1 percent of NNP averaged in the 1950's and 1960's.\n435:[Omitted long matching line]\n437:Net domestic investment equaled 9.2 percent of NNP in the first half of 1998. This was up from a low 4.9 percent for all of 1991 and was the highest reading in more than a decade. About one-quarter of investment so far this year was financed by foreign inflows, which were equivalent to 2.6 percent of NNP. U.S. sources provided investment equal to 6.6 percent of NNP, only a little more than half as much as averaged in the 1950's and 1960's.\n489:The following capsule analysis of budget receipts, by source, for the third quarter of fiscal 1998 supplements fiscal data reported in the September issue of the \"Treasury Bulletin.\" At the time of that issue's release, not enough data were available to analyze adequately collections for the quarter.\n491:[Omitted long matching line]\n493:Corporation income taxes\u2014Net corporate income tax receipts were $70.4 billion for the third quarter of fiscal 1998. State deposits decreased $0.9 billion to $9.8 billion. Federal unemployment taxes increased $0.2 billion to $3.4 billion. Railroad receipts increased negligibly.\n501:Contributions for other insurance and retirement\u2014Contributions for other retirement were $1.1 billion for the third quarter of fiscal 1998. There was a negligible increase over receipts from the comparable quarter of fiscal 1997. The growth in contributions will remain flat over the next few years as the number of employees covered by the Federal employees\u2019 retirement system (FERS) grows slowly relative to those covered under the civil service retirement system (CSRS).\n503:Excise taxes\u2014Net excise tax receipts for the third quarter of fiscal 1998 were $16.0 billion, an increase of $1.2 billion over the comparable prior year quarter.     2. Propose a skill from the failed traces¶

    Sonnet receives the missed answers and their observable traces, without the gold answers. Its structured output is just a name and a high-level description. If every answer passes, the next three cells skip the unnecessary revision.

    proposal = None
if failures:
    proposal = proposer(json.dumps({"skills": empty_skills, "failures": failures})).require()
    display(JSON(proposal.output.model_dump()))
else:
    print("All three answers passed; no failed trace needs a skill.")

    proposer: starting
proposer: complete

    {
  "description": "Method for answering numeric questions from OCR'd or text-converted Treasury Bulletin and other government financial tables. Locate the right table and edition, and prefer the most recent or authoritative edition for the year asked. Realign columns when a wide table is split into blocks with shifted or unnamed headers. Handle department subtotals (for example, a department split into sub-columns) and footnote markers. Check units and fiscal versus calendar year. Cross-check with a second table or edition. Then give a clean final answer: a plain number in the requested units, plus the entity, table and source used.",
  "name": "treasury-bulletin-table-lookup"
}

    3. Build the proposed skill¶

    The generator invokes its initialized skill-builder, then writes the proposed folder. Inspect the rendered skill below. The expandable trace shows that the builder was actually invoked; generated_skills validates what changed.

    if proposal:
    generated = generator(proposal.output.model_dump_json()).require()
    seed = dict(generated_skills(empty_skills, proposal.output, generated, skill_repo))
    display(Markdown(skill_markdown(seed)))
    display(HTML(transcripts([generated])))
else:
    seed = empty_skills

    generator: starting
generator: complete
    Read the generated SKILL.md

    .claude/skills/treasury-bulletin-table-lookup/SKILL.md

    name: treasury-bulletin-table-lookup
description: Method for answering numeric questions from OCR'd or text-converted Treasury Bulletin and other government financial tables. Use it to pick the right table and edition, realign shifted columns in wide split tables, handle department subtotals and footnote markers, check units and fiscal versus calendar year, cross-check, and give a clean final answer.
    Treasury Bulletin table lookup

    Use with Read, Glob and Grep only. Do the arithmetic by hand and show it briefly.

    1. Parse the question first Write down, before searching: - Entity (agency, department, fund, security type, account). - Measure (receipts, outlays, debt, holdings, yield, and so on). - Period: year, month, quarter, and whether it is fiscal or calendar. - Requested units (millions, billions, percent) and any calculation (sum, difference, change, percent change, average).

    2. Find the table and edition 1. Grep for the table title or distinctive row labels, such as the entity name, "Table OFS-1", "FFO-", or "Budget Receipts and Outlays". Use several synonyms. OCR often breaks words or case. 2. List the candidate files with Glob. Identify each file's edition (month and year) from its header or filename. 3. Pick the edition by these rules: - Data for a period normally appears in the edition published shortly after it. Fiscal year-end data usually appears in the September or December edition, or a later edition that carries annual tables. - If several editions report the same figure, prefer the most recent edition. It has revisions. Use an earlier edition only if the question names it, or if it is the only one that shows the figure. - For a historical annual series, check the table that lists that year as a column or row. Do not assume the edition year equals the data year. 4. Read the table's title, subtitle, units line ("In millions of dollars") and footnotes. Do this before reading any numbers.

    3. Read the right cell - Wide tables are often split into blocks (part 1 with columns A–F, part 2 with columns G–L), or the header line is shifted from the data lines. Count the columns. - Count the number of values in the row. Compare that with the number of header names. Align from the right edge for totals and from the left edge for labels. - If a header cell is empty or unnamed, check whether it belongs to a spanning header, such as a department that has sub-columns. Check the neighbouring rows and the total column. Sub-columns plus the remainder should sum to the total. - Verify the alignment with a check. Pick a row whose total is known and confirm that the parts add up to it. If they do not, shift the alignment by one and retest. - Department subtotals: a department may appear as a total row followed by indented component rows, or as a group of columns. Take the row or column the question names. Do not double-count the subtotal and its components when summing. - Footnote markers (1/, (a), , r, p, e) that are attached to numbers are not digits. "r" means revised and "p" means preliminary. Strip the marker, but note it. - Read negatives written as parentheses or minus signs, and dashes or "" for zero or less than 0.5 unit. Watch for OCR errors: "O" and "0", "l" and "1", a missing decimal point, or commas replaced by periods. Flag a value that breaks the pattern of its row or column. - Confirm that the period label matches (for example the "Fiscal year" versus the "Calendar year" columns, or the month columns at the right of the table).

    4. Units and periods - Convert only when the question asks for different units. Millions to billions: divide by 1,000. Say which conversion you applied. - Fiscal year (US federal): October to September. Calendar year: January to December. If the table gives a fiscal-year figure and the question asks for calendar year (or the reverse), find a table that gives the correct basis. Otherwise state the assumption. - Percent versus percentage points. Rounding: report to the precision the question asks for. If none is stated, use the table's precision, or two decimals for a computed ratio.

    5. Cross-check (do at least one) - Find the same figure in a second table in the same edition (for example a summary table versus a detail table), or in the next edition, and compare. Explain any difference by revision, or by a definitional difference. - Check that components add to the reported total. - Sanity-check the magnitude against neighbouring years or rows.

    6. Compute Show the formula with the numbers you read, and do the arithmetic step by step. Re-add or re-divide once to check it.

    7. Final answer Give: - The plain number in the requested units, with no footnote markers, and with commas only if they are natural. Include the unit (for example "$12,345 million") when it is unambiguous. - One line naming the entity, the table (title and number), the edition (month and year), and the row and column used. Add a note if you made an assumption or the editions differ.

    Stop when the answer has been read from the right cell, cross-checked, and stated. If the data truly are absent from every edition you can find, say so and give the closest available figure, labelled as such. Do not guess.

S. Government Printing Office, Washington 25, D. C. Subscription per year $6.00 domestic, $7.50 foreign. Single copy price varies.\n29\t\n30\tOctober 1958\n31\t\n32\tI\n33\t\n34\tTable of Contents\n35\t\n36\t| 0 | 1 |\n37\t| --- | --- |\n38\t| Treasury financing operations | A-1 |\n39\t| Summary of Federal fiscal operations | 1 |\n40\t| Budget receipts and expenditures | 2 |\n41\t| Trust account and other transactions | 8 |\n42\t| Cash income and outgo | 12 |\n43\t| Account of the Treasurer of the United States | 18 |\n44\t| Debt outstanding | 20 |\n45\t| Statutory debt limitation | 23 |\n46\t| Debt operations | 24 |\n47\t| United States savings bonds | 36 |\n48\t| Ownership of Federal securities | 40 |\n49\t| Treasury survey of ownership of Federal securities | 42 |\n50\t| Market quotations on Treasury securities | 46 |\n51\t| Average yields of long-term bonds | 49 |\n52\t| Internal revenue collections | 51 |\n53\t| Monetary statistics | 53 |\n54\t| Capital movements | 57 |\n55\t| Cumulative table of contents | 68 |\n56\t\n57\tNote: Where calculations have been made from unrounded figures, the details may not check to the totals shown.\n58\t\n59\tII\n60\t\n61\tTreasury Bulletin\n62\t\n63\tReporting Bases\n64\t\n65\tData on receipts, expenditures, and debt which appear in the \"Treasury Bulletin\" are based largely on two Treasury financial reports, the \"Daily Statement of the United States Treasury\" and the \"Monthly Statement of Receipts and Expenditures of the United States Government.\" Certain monetary statistics are based at least in part on the \"Circulation Statement of United States Money.\" Where these statements are given as sources for individual tables, they are cited by name only. Their respective reporting bases are described below. For other data in the Bulletin, information on sources or reporting bases is given in connection with the tables themselves.\n66\t\n67\tThe monthly statement of receipts and expenditures was first published for February 1954, and replaced the daily statement as the primary source of information on budget results and other receipt and expenditure data classified by type of account. At the same time, the daily statement was changed to a statement of cash deposits and withdrawals affecting the account of the Treasurer of the United States. Both publications have provided comparative figures on their respective bases from the beginning of the fiscal year 1953. The announcement of February 17, 1954, with respect to these reporting changes may be found in the April 1954 issue of the Bulletin.\n68\t\n69\tThe monthly statement shows all receipts and expenditures of the Government, including those made from cash accounts held outside the United States Treasury. The information is compiled from reports by the Treasurer of the United States and by all other collecting and disbursing agencies, including those agencies which maintain checking accounts in commercial banks. These reports cover transactions recorded in the accounts of the agencies during the reporting period. The net of the transactions as compiled from these reports is reconciled in the monthly statement to changes in the balance in the Treasurer's account and in cash held outside the Treasurer's account and changes in the public debt outstanding.\n70\t\n71\tReceipts of taxes and customs duties are reported on a collections basis. Other receipts are reported partially on a collections basis and partially on a deposits basis. Expenditures, except interest on the public debt, are reported on the basis of checks issued or cash payments made by disburseing officers. Transactions of an intrfund or intragovernmental nature are included on the same basis even though the actual issuance of checks may not be involved. Interest on the public debt is included on an accrual basis beginning with figures for June 1955 and the fiscal year 1955. Prior to that, it was included on a due and payable basis. The same reporting basis as that in the monthly statement provides the fiscal year figures for the Treasury's \"Combined Statement of Receipts, Expenditures and Balances of the United States Government\" and for actual receipts and expenditures in the \"Budget of the United States Government.\"\n72\t\n73\tThe daily statement on the new basis was first issued for February 17, 1954. In the deposits and withdrawals as shown, no distinction is made as to the type of accounts (budget, trust, etc.). The deposits are on the basis of certificates of deposit cleared through the account of the Treasurer of the United States. Total withdrawals are on the basis of checks paid or cash disbursements made out of the Treasurer's account. Some of the withdrawal classifications shown are reported on the basis of mailed reports of checks issued and are adjusted by means of clearing accounts to the total of checks paid. Except for relatively minor amounts, noncash interfund and other intragovernmental transactions are excluded. The public debt figures in the daily statement also are on a \"clearance\" basis, with the exception of those issuance and retirement transactions reported on the basis of telegrams from Federal Reserve Banks. Noncash debt transactions are included, however.\n74\t\n75\tThe daily statement before February 17, 1954, covered not only transactions cleared through the Treasurer's account but also certain Government agency transactions which were handled through commercial bank accounts, and included noncash interfund and other intragovernmental transactions. It provided information similar to that in the present daily statement with respect to the status of the Treasurer's account, and similar to that in the present end-of-month daily statement with respect to debt issuance, retirement, and amount outstanding. Receipts and expenditures, however, were classified by type of account, and the budget results shown in the daily statement were used as the basis for reflecting the results under the President's budget program as enacted by the Congress.\n76\t\n77\tReceipts were on the basis of deposits as they cleared the Treasurer's account. Expenditures cleared through the Treasurer's account were reported on two successive bases. Through 1946 they were on the basis of checks paid by the Treasurer of the United States. Beginning with 1947, expenditure made through the facilities of the Treasury Department's Division of Disbursement were on the basis of checks issued, while certain others, principally those of the Department of Defense and its predecessor organizations, were on the basis of checks paid. Transactions handled through commercial bank accounts, consisting of market transactions in public debt and guaranteed securities, were as reported by the agencies. Interest on the public debt was included on a due and payable basis beginning with November 1949 and on a checks-paid basis prior to that time.\n78\t\n79\tThe circulation statement reflects transactions through the Treasurer's account which affect monetary stocks of gold and silver and the amounts of coin and currency in the money supply of the country. It is issued later than the daily statement, however, and the figures are based on transactions consummated during the reporting period even though some may not have cleared the Treasurer's account during that period.\n80\t\n81\tOctober 1958\n82\t\n83\tA-1\n84\t\n85\tTreasury Financing Operations\n86\t\n87\tSeptember Cash Offering\n88\t\n89\tAn offering on September 29 of two securities for cash subscription was announced by the Treasury Department on September 25. An issue of 3-1/2 per-cent 13-month Treasury notes was offered for $1 billion, or thereabouts, and an issue of 219-day special Treasury bills, for $2-1/2 billion, or thereabouts. The bills were issued on a fixed-price basis, priced at 98.023, to yield approximately 3.25 percent per annum. In addition, it was stated that up to $100 million of the notes might be allotted to Government investment accounts. The subscription books were open only on September 29.\n90\t\n91\tAllotments on subscriptions to the notes totaled $1,184 million, including $100 million allotted to Government investment accounts. Subscriptions received amounted to nearly $2.7 billion, of which 79 percent came from others than commercial banks. Subscriptions in excess of $50,000 were allotted at 35 percent, but not less than $50,000. Subscriptions of $50,000 or less were allotted in full.\n92\t\n93\tAllotments on the subscriptions to the 219-day bills amounted to $2,735 million. Subscriptions received totaled nearly $5.5 billion, or which 17 percent were received from nonbank subscribers. Subscriptions in excess of $100,000 were allotted 44 percent, but not less than $100,000 and those of $100,000 or less were allotted in full.\n94\t\n95\tCommercial banks, which for this purpose are defined as banks accepting demand deposits, were permitted to subscribe for their own account without deposit, but for the notes were restricted to an amount not exceeding 25 percent and for the bills to an amount not exceeding 50 percent of the combined capital, surplus, and undivided profits of the subscribing bank. Any commercial bank qualified as a depositary was permitted to make payment for the securities allotted to itself and to its customers by credit in its Treasury tax and loan account up to the amount for which it was qualified in excess of existing deposits. A payment of 2 per-\n96\t\n97\tcent of the amount of the securities applied for was required on all other subscriptions.\n98\t\n99\tCommercial banks and other lenders were requested to refrain from making unsecured loans, or loans collateralized in whole or in part by the securities subscribed for, to cover the 2 percent deposits required to be paid when subscriptions were entered. Commercial banks were required to certify on all subscriptions entered for account of their customers that no such loans had been made; that the banks had no beneficial interest in their customers' subscriptions; and that the subscriptions for their own account were not in excess of the stipulated percentages of their capital, surplus, and undivided profits.\n100\t",

    5. Continue with a population of three¶

    Longer step. seed is the folder just generated, captured as a path-to-text dictionary. No SkillSet is needed; snapshots are validated with SourceTree. Two small callbacks repeat the calls above for whichever parent the search selects.

    Here proposer names the inner agent that proposes a skill plan. The outer revise function combines that call with generator, which builds the files. Meta-Evolve receives the complete workflow as proposer=revise; search chooses the parent folder, and revise decides how to change it.

    The search grades that seed on all three development questions and tries up to two further revisions. Population(size=3) retains up to three folders, selecting parents through tournaments of two. A perfect score stops early. The final table shows each candidate's parent, score and frontier membership, including regressions. Wrong answers score zero; failed SDK calls or grading remain unrankable failures.

    def evaluate(skills, split="development"):
    restore(skills, skill_repo)
    questions = data.cases(split)
    answers = [executor(case["question"]) for case in questions]
    display(Markdown(answer_table(questions, answers, data.score_answer)))
    return evaluation(questions, answers, data.score_answer, split)

def revise(skills, *, context):
    observed = context.evidence.latest("evoskill.development")
    restore(skills, skill_repo)
    proposal = proposer(json.dumps({"skills": dict(skills), "failures": failure_feedback(observed.data)}))
    generated = generator(proposal.output.model_dump_json()) if not proposal.failure else None
    revision = skill_revision(skills, proposal, generated, skill_repo, (observed.ref,))
    print("Proposed:", proposal.output if not proposal.failure else proposal.failure)
    return revision

experiment = meta.Experiment(
    task=meta.Task(evaluator=evaluate, objectives=(meta.Maximize("solved", satisfy=1.0),)),
    seed=seed, proposer=revise,
    search=meta.Population(size=3, tournament_size=2, max_trials=2),
    context=meta.RecentAncestors(max_records=24, max_chars=12000), random_seed=0,
)
run = meta.run(experiment)
display(Markdown(population_table(run)))

    6. Check three held-out questions¶

    Compare the original empty folder and the selected folder on UID0001 (1940 defense spending), UID0002 (1934 Veterans Administration spending), and UID0006 (largest country's claims in 1995). Their answers never go to the proposer. Both versions search the same nine Treasury documents with the same Haiku agent and scorer. The tables show exactly which questions each version solved.

    if run.summary().primary_score is not None:
    winner = dict(run.best().value)
    print("Empty folder — held out")
    baseline = evaluate(empty_skills, "held_out")
    print("Selected folder — held out")
    selected = baseline if winner == empty_skills else evaluate(winner, "held_out")
    restore(winner, skill_repo)
    report = save_report(run, data, baseline, selected)
    display(JSON({key: report[key] for key in (
        "held_out_baseline", "held_out_selected", "held_out_failures", "sdk_calls", "estimated_cost_usd",
    )}))

    Reading this capture. The manual retry corrects a missed answer. Population search keeps its starting skill folder after the further revisions; the original empty folder and selected folder both solve 3/3 held-out questions. This demonstrates the workflow and a corrected retry, with no measured held-out accuracy gain.

    The run folder contains the selected skills, full observable SDK receipts, held-out answers and report.json. run.usage() covers the population stage; the report counts every call, including the walkthrough and held-out comparison. SDK dollar estimates are separate from unknown billed spend. Search history is in memory; receipts remain on disk. These six questions teach the loop, not benchmark gains. Model outputs and discovered skills can vary between runs.

    To keep experimenting, edit a skill in skill_repo/.claude/skills/, set seed = dict(snapshot(skill_repo)), and rerun the population cell.

    This follows EvoSkill's proposal/generation flow; see the paper for the full research setup. The public sample derives from OfficeQA (CC-BY-SA-4.0), with the UID0003 clarification above. Its Apache-2.0 scorer is unchanged.
